CONTINUOUS-TIME MOTION PLANNING
AND STEERING WITH GLOBAL CONVERGENCE
Mohsen Amiri†, Sima Didari†, Davoud Ataee Tarzanagh†, Hankyu Moon, Yeongdae Kwon
Samsung SDS Research America, Mountain View, CA, USA
Project Page
ABSTRACT
Motion planning is central to robotics, and it is increasingly used to correct the
motions proposed by learned planners and policies. In both uses, constraints must
hold at every instant of the motion, not only at sampled times. To our knowledge,
no algorithm provably converges to a globally optimal motion once a smoothness
cost is combined with such constraints. We cast planning and steering as a single
continuous-time program: robots avoid one another and static or moving obsta-
cles, and a learned proposal is steered by keeping the motion close to it. The
program is elementary to state, yet nonconvex. We propose GlobMP (Global Mo-
tion Planning), a gradient projection method over piecewise-polynomial curves,
on which each constraint is one polynomial per piece that must stay nonnegative
over the whole motion; it alternates cost reduction with feasibility restoration. Un-
der mild assumptions, we show that, from a fine grid start, its cost approaches the
global optimum as the number of polynomial pieces grows, with every constraint
met to any chosen tolerance, or exactly on stable instances. We demonstrate its
superior performance over strong baselines in real and synthetic experiments, in-
cluding teams of up to 200 robots, multi-robot planning on the Robotarium testbed
and an extended benchmark, maze navigation, and steering tactile-reactive poli-
cies on a bimanual humanoid.
1
INTRODUCTION
Motion planning is a fundamental problem in robotics: it asks for a trajectory that connects a given
initial state to a goal while satisfying motion constraints, such as avoiding collisions (LaValle, 1998).
It underlies navigation, manipulation (Sundaralingam & Hermans, 2019; Gao et al., 2024; Amice
et al., 2024), multi-robot coordination (Li et al., 2021; Tang et al., 2025; Shaoul et al., 2025), and,
increasingly, the steering of learned planners and policies (Janner et al., 2022; Carvalho et al., 2023;
Chi et al., 2023; Giaretta et al., 2026).
Motion planners approach this problem in several ways, each with a gap. Sampling-based planners
explore many routes (Karaman & Frazzoli, 2011), yet smoothing their waypoints can introduce
collisions; optimization-based planners improve a motion locally (Zucker et al., 2013; Tedrake & the
Drake Development Team, 2019), with TrajOpt (Schulman et al., 2014) also supporting continuous
collision checks. Constraints enforced at sampled times do not ensure feasibility between them
(Hargraves & Paris, 1987; Figure 1). Convex-region planners search a decomposition of free space
(Deits & Tedrake, 2015a; Marcucci et al., 2023), and semidefinite relaxations certify a polynomial
motion as globally optimal only when the relaxation is tight (Graesdal et al., 2026). Even shortest-
path complexity depends on the geometry: polynomial-time in the real-RAM model among planar
discs (Chang et al., 2005), NP-hard among polyhedra in R3 (Canny & Reif, 1987), and still open in
R3 even among disjoint unit discs or axis-aligned unit cubes (Mitchell, 2017).
Our setting is a point robot in Rn that travels between prescribed boundary states around obstacles
while minimizing a smoothness cost; steering a learned planner or policy is the same problem with
one added constraint that keeps the robot near its proposal. Although elementary to state, this setting
already contains the core difficulty of avoiding collisions; to our knowledge, no algorithm converges
to its global optimum once a derivative cost such as acceleration or snap is combined with collision

search over initial curves. We consider

Here γ : [0,1] →Rn is the robot’s trajectory, with time normalized to [0,1], and H k = H k([0,1];Rn)
denotes the Sobolev space of curves whose derivatives through order k are square-integrable: the
objective is finite exactly on H k, and its curves have k−1 continuous derivatives, so the constraints
and boundary values are defined at every instant. The objective measures smoothness through the kth
derivative: k = 2 penalizes acceleration and k = 4 snap (Mellinger & Kumar, 2011). The collision
constraints keep the robot at distance at least rj > 0 from each of p obstacle centres cj(t), which may
move, and the steering constraint keeps it within ¯r > 0 of a reference ρ(t), both at every instant. The
boundary conditions fix the position and its derivatives through order ℓat both ends. Unlike Graesdal
et al. (2026), whose spheres are static, we let the centres move in time; this single extension covers
several robots, each a moving centre for the others, and steering, where the robot must stay near the
moving centre ρ(t).
Several robots are planned by stacking their curves into one γ and keeping robots a and b (radii
ra,rb) apart: ∥γ a(t)−γ b(t)∥≥ra +rb (Shaoul et al., 2025). The reference ρ can be the waypoints
of a learned planner (Janner et al., 2022) or the action chunk of a policy (Chi et al., 2023), which the
steering constraint corrects rather than replaces; with ¯r = ∞it is dropped and (P∞) is plain motion
planning. Boundary orders s ≥k bind only our polynomial curves (Assumption A). Ellipsoids, walls,
corridors and other variants are in Appendix A2.1.
2


Our contributions address two difficulties of (P∞): constraints must hold at every instant, and im-
proving one feasible curve need not find the best route.
C1. We enforce collision constraints exactly in continuous time. At a fixed polynomial degree,
each constraint becomes a univariate polynomial that must be nonnegative on [0,1] (Powers &
Reznick, 2000), so it holds between sample times too; collision, robot separation and steering
share one block formulation (§2).
C2. We propose GlobMP, a gradient projection algorithm that alternates a cost step with feasibil-
ity restoration (§2). Cone projections give coefficient corrections for violated blocks, which
Jacobians turn into control-point updates, block by block and with the boundary states kept.
C3. We prove global convergence (Theorem 1): GlobMP keeps every curve feasible without in-
creasing its cost and, from a fine grid start with N polynomial pieces, approaches the best
continuous-time motion as N grows, within any constraint tolerance and exactly on stable in-
stances.
C4. We deploy GlobMP on real robots and show superior performance over strong baselines: multi-
robot teams on the Robotarium testbed (§4.2), and steering state-of-the-art expert policies on
a bimanual humanoid with dexterous hands in tactile manipulation tasks (§4.3). The appendix
adds an extended multi-robot benchmark, maze navigation and end-effector obstacle avoidance
on D3IL (Appendix A4).
2
POLYNOMIAL FORMULATION AND GRADIENT PROJECTION
We optimize the control points of a polynomial curve, alternating a gradient step on the cost with
restoration of feasibility; because the curve is a polynomial, each constraint is checked over the
whole motion. We represent the curve by a polynomial of degree d (piecewise when the horizon is
split) in the Bernstein basis [bd(t)]i =

where Dπ j is the Jacobian (matrix of first derivatives) in free coordinates and the gradient is reshaped

feasible or nearest curve in finitely many steps; the theory analyses the repair below.
Each cone projection is a small convex problem. Evaluating a polynomial at time t is an inner
product of its coefficients with bm j(t), so the vectors bmj(t), t ∈[0,1], generate the dual cone K ∗

iteration grows linearly with the number of blocks at fixed degree and cone-solve iterations.

adds pieces (Theorem 1) or raises the degree (Algorithm 4, under a condition on configurations).
3
THEORY
We state one theorem, for (P∞); its fixed-degree counterpart and all proofs are in Appendix A3. Let
E⋆
∞denote the optimal value of (P∞).
Assumption A (problem data). The cost order is k ≥1 and the endpoints fix derivatives of orders
0,...,ℓ(if ℓ≥k, (P∞) keeps those of order below k); the obstacle centres and the reference are
continuous and piecewise polynomial in t; the endpoints are strictly feasible; and (P∞) is feasible.
Assumption B (enough boundary conditions). The two endpoints fix at least as many values per
coordinate as the order of the cost: 2(ℓ+1) ≥k.
A change of the curve that keeps the boundary values and has zero kth-derivative cost is a polynomial
of degree below k with a root of order ℓ+ 1 at each endpoint, which is zero under Assumption B.
Hence f is strongly convex, with curvature at most β = 2λmax(H), H = N⊤QN. For |˜r| ≤˜rmax,
˜rmax > 0 below every radius and half the endpoint clearance (Appendix A3.2.2), let (P˜r
∞) denote
(P∞) with every obstacle radius raised by ˜r, every pair separation by 2˜r and ¯r lowered by ˜r (˜r < 0, a
tolerance, loosens them), and E⋆
∞(˜r) its optimal value.
Let SN denote the curves of N polynomial pieces of a fixed degree d ≥max{2k −1,ℓ} on equal
intervals, joined with k −1 continuous derivatives. Let zh denote a cheapest point of the grid of
spacing h in their free control points that is feasible for the problem being solved; grid points can be
checked exactly by root isolation for rational data.

In minimum-snap planning (k = 4) with position through jerk fixed at both ends (ℓ= 3), accuracy

cost of order 1/N at the end pieces, hence order ε−1 pieces.
4
EXPERIMENTS
We evaluate GlobMP on synthetic teams of 2 to 200 robots among spheres (§4.1), a multi-robot
benchmark on the Robotarium testbed (§4.2) and tactile-reactive manipulation (§4.3). Algorithm 2
restores from heuristic seeds (route classes, prioritized plans, the executing curve).
4.1
SYNTHETIC EXPERIMENTS
The synthetic study (Problem 1, Appendix A4.2) has robots exchange places in pairs past static
spheres, under the minimum-snap cost with piecewise degree-9 polynomials; each pair or sphere
can be passed on either side, so an instance has many route classes. A curve is feasible if it makes no
contact at any t; GlobMP keeps a 0.5 cm margin. GlobMP runs Algorithms 1–2 from one waypoint
path per route class and keeps the cheapest feasible result. The baselines are RRT-Connect (Kuffner
& LaValle, 2000), RRT∗(Karaman & Frazzoli, 2011) and GCS (Marcucci et al., 2023) on IRIS
regions (Deits & Tedrake, 2015a) in the joint configuration space, whose paths are fitted by the
cheapest collision-free (else least-colliding) curve in GlobMP’s class (Richter et al., 2016), and
SDP-MP of Graesdal et al. (2026) on the same program (Appendix A4.2).
Each baseline imposes the constraints off the curve it returns (along a sampled path, inside convex
regions, or on a relaxed variable), keeps centimetres to decimetres of slack, and fails more often as
robots are added; SDP-MP collides or times out on every instance (Figure 2a,b). GlobMP is feasible
at every t on every instance and ends at the margin. Feasible baseline curves cost from about twice
to hundreds of times more, with a gap that mostly grows with team size (Figure 2c); where SDP-MP
returns a lower bound, GlobMP is within 6% of it at the median. Theorem 1 covers this curve class
(ℓ= k, rate N−1) but not its seeds, so the lowest cost found is not a proven optimum. From 2 to 200
robots GlobMP is feasible at every t on every seed, at 200 robots (82,000 blocks) in two minutes on
CPU and 28 s on one GPU from straight lines (Figure 3a,b), with time per iteration at most linear in
the blocks (Figure 3c); the baselines are feasible on at most 2 of 5 seeds at eight robots and on none
from 16.
To check the rates of Theorem 1 and Corollary 2, we solve one two-robot instance again and again
with more pieces N and track how far GlobMP’s best cost stays above the limit (Figure 4). When
the ends fix every derivative up to the one in the cost, the extra cost halves each time N doubles,

for velocity, acceleration, jerk and snap: this is the cost of the end pieces in the discussion after
Corollary 2, and the bound is tight for k ≥2. With one derivative fewer fixed and k ≥2, the extra
cost falls much faster than the corollary’s bound and is below the numerical precision by N = 8 for
jerk, N = 10 for snap and N = 20 for acceleration, so the bound holds but is loose here. For k ≥2
the cost grows linearly with the obstacle margin, so the rate (c+1)δN after Theorem 1 fits.
4.2
MULTI-ROBOT MOTION PLANNING
Multi-robot motion planning coordinates the simultaneous motion of several robots toward as-
signed goals while avoiding collisions with obstacles and with one another. We evaluate GlobMP
on five multi-robot environments, Empty, Conveyor, Cross-Room, Drop-Region and
Corridor-Bay, from Shaoul et al. (2025) (Cross-Room and Corridor-Bay reconstructed
from Liang et al. 2025), following the test-time velocity constraint and collision predicate of Giaretta
et al. (2026) on the Georgia Tech Robotarium (Wilson et al., 2020), with teams of Na ∈{3,6,9,12}
robots. The environments are fitted to the 3.2×2.0 m Robotarium arena, with the common velocity
limit selected for the Robotarium setting and inflated robot radius r = 0.13 m. For each (map, team-
size) pair, we use ten start–goal instances: nine sampled from the benchmark distribution and one
“ring” instance across the workspace (Conveyor below Na = 12: ten sampled). Hardware details
of the Robotarium setup are provided in Appendix A4.1.

Figure 4: Relative extra cost over the limit as the number of pieces grows (one two-robot instance),
for velocity to snap (k = 1,...,4). (a) One derivative fewer fixed at the ends; dotted: rates of
Corollary 2; shaded: numerical precision. (b) The cost’s own derivative also fixed; dashed: halving
per doubling. (c) Extra cost against the margin ˜r/r, with r the smaller sphere radius.
GlobMP needs no training: at test time it solves each instance directly as one (P), with the robots’
curves stacked, a collision constraint at radius 2r for every pair and affine blocks for boxes (Ap-
pendix A2.1.1), so its plan is collision-free at every t. The baselines are CD, PCD-LB and PCD-
SHD (Luan et al., 2026), Final Proj. and DiRecT (Giaretta et al., 2026) and MMD-CBS (Shaoul
et al., 2025). Each method is evaluated on the same start–goal instances. The sampling baselines
draw 64 candidates, whereas GlobMP returns one trajectory.
Figure 5 reports the success of a single returned plan across the five environments and team sizes.
On Conveyor and Cross-Room, baseline performance drops rapidly as the number of robots
increases: every baseline reaches zero on Conveyor at Na = 12 and stays at or below 0.50 on
Cross-Room from Na = 9, whereas GlobMP’s single plan succeeds on every instance except
two of the ten on Conveyor at Na = 12. The stronger projection baselines remain more reli-
able on Empty, Drop-Region, and Corridor-Bay, but most also degrade with team size.
Figure 6 presents representative 12-robot ring trajectories on Cross-Room and Conveyor. Ap-
pendix A4.5 extends the evaluation to eight environments, teams of up to 21 robots, three velocity
caps, and a larger set of start–goal instances.
4.3
STEERING TACTILE-REACTIVE DEXTEROUS POLICIES
A Dexmate Vega-1 with two XHAND1 hands with fingertip force sensing (Appendix A4.1) per-
forms four bimanual tasks learned from teleoperation (Figure 7a). The expert policies are two state-
of-the-art vision-language-action models, GR00T N1.7 (NVIDIA, 2026), built on the GR00T N1
architecture (NVIDIA et al., 2025), and π0.5 (Physical Intelligence et al., 2025), each with and with-
out tactile input. Both command absolute joint targets for both arms and hands (n = 38) at 30 Hz in
chunks of 16 executed steps.
Deployed, the policies fail geometrically (they replay memorized targets), temporally (consecu-
tive chunks disagree by up to 0.5 rad) and at contact, mostly because of observations outside the
training distribution, which no motion constraint can observe; so we separate deciding which con-
straints apply from enforcing them (Figure 7b). A meta-reasoner, the vision-language model Claude
Opus 5.5 (Anthropic, 2026), defines the constraints once, at reset, from the images, the task and the
measured state, for example the keep-out, keep-in and grip of Figure 7b. A certifier then solves
(P) per chunk, with the policy’s chunk as the reference ρ, so the executed curve meets every active
constraint at every t. Keep-out and keep-in blocks act on hand positions, which depend on the joints
through forward kinematics; the certifier enforces them by linearizing the kinematics at the current
curve and re-solving, so for these blocks feasibility at every t holds up to that linearization, while
blocks on the joints themselves, such as the grip, are exact. The meta-reasoner only chooses con-
straints and never solves them, so a wrong choice changes which feasible curve is returned, never
whether it is feasible, unlike model-written programs (Huang et al., 2024). If the policy slows, a
demonstrated time law rescales the reference (Kant & Zucker, 1986).

Figure 6: Executed Robotarium trajectories for the 12-robot ring instance on Cross-Room and
Conveyor. Obstacle-hit counts span the whole run; circles magnify highlighted regions.
The baselines are the expert policies alone, with and without touch (none with touch on the tissue),
and every condition run has 9 or 10 real trials per task. Steering matches or raises every task (Fig-
ure 7c): it turns the tissue, which the policies alone rarely complete, into a mostly successful task,
and completes the handover in every trial. Touch pays off where the hand must regulate contact, as
in placing the toy, pulling the tissue and stowing the mug, and adds little where the task is guided
by vision, as in the handover, where it even hurts the policies alone. In a closed-loop simulator
replaying failed real tissue deployments, the pinch lands where and when demonstrated only with
steering.
5
CONCLUSION
We developed GlobMP, a gradient projection method with convex feasibility restoration for
continuous-time motion planning over polynomial curves; it enforces every collision constraint at
every instant, with planning and steering in one program. Under mild assumptions and from a
fine grid start, its cost approaches the continuous-time optimum as the number of pieces grows,
within any constraint tolerance and exactly on stable instances. In real and synthetic experiments,
from teams of up to 200 robots to the Robotarium testbed and tactile manipulation on a bimanual
humanoid, it outperforms strong baselines. Extending the guarantee to speed limits and corridor
constraints is a natural next step.

AI USE STATEMENT
We disclose the use of large language models (LLMs) to aid and polish the writing, editing the text
for clarity and length; for retrieval and discovery, helping find related work and check citations; and
to generate the synthetic datasets, whose instances are produced by LLM-assisted scripts from fixed
seeds (Appendix A4.2). The authors take full responsibility for all content.
REPRODUCIBILITY STATEMENT
We will release all code: the GlobMP solver (restoration and projected gradient steps), the scripts
and configurations for every experiment and figure, and the steering and deployment code for learned
policies on the Vega-1 with XHAND1 hands (certifier, grounding, time law, policy servers), together
with the teleoperation demonstrations and fine-tuned policy checkpoints of §4.3. The project page,
https://GlobMP.github.io/, collects the videos and results.

A1
ADDENDUM TO SECTION 1: RELATED WORK AND FIGURE 1
A1.1
EXTENDED RELATED WORK
Relation to existing formulations. Collocation replaces each block by inequalities at sampled
times (Hargraves & Paris, 1987); the control-polygon rule asks every coefficient to be nonnegative
(Gao et al., 2018), which is sufficient for Kj but conservative; convex decompositions (Deits &
16


Tedrake, 2015a; Marcucci et al., 2023) are exact for the decomposition rather than for the free space;
semidefinite relaxations (Lasserre, 2001; Parrilo, 2003; Graesdal et al., 2026) certify a trajectory only
when tight. We keep the original variables and free space, test the exact condition on each block,
and approach the optimum of (P∞) as the class is refined, within any constraint tolerance and exactly
on stable instances (Theorem 1); under a condition on configurations (Assumption C) we also reach
global minimizers over each fine enough spline class (Proposition 13).
Sampling-based planning and trajectory optimization. The two classical families both act on the
nonconvex problem itself, and neither certifies global optimality. Sampling-based planners (LaValle,
1998; Karaman & Frazzoli, 2011) are probabilistically complete and, for RRT*, asymptotically
optimal, but in probability and only to the robust optimum (Solovey et al., 2020), the least cost
approached with a positive clearance, which can exceed the optimum. Trajectory optimizers instead
discretize and hand the result to local nonlinear programming: direct collocation (Hargraves & Paris,
1987), covariant gradient descent (Zucker et al., 2013), sequential convexification (Schulman et al.,
2014), and differentiable collision geometry (Tracy et al., 2023); this pipeline is fast and widely
deployed but converges to a stationary point with no test for whether it is global. We keep this
setting and add a proof of global convergence.
Convex decomposition and graphs of convex sets. A second line makes the problem tractable by
convexifying the domain. IRIS inflates large obstacle-free convex regions (Deits & Tedrake, 2015a),
then used for mixed-integer planning (Deits & Tedrake, 2015b); shortest-paths-in-graphs-of-convex-
sets then plans over that decomposition as a mixed-integer convex program, solved through its con-
vex relaxation and rounding (Marcucci et al., 2024; 2023), with subsequent work on scalability
(Marcucci et al., 2025; Werner et al., 2026; 2025), on space-time variants for multiple robots (Tang
et al., 2025), and on using the convex solution to warm-start nonconvex trajectory optimization (von
Wrangel & Tedrake, 2024). These methods bound their optimality gap for the decomposition: the
regions are a modelling choice made before optimizing, and a curve is admissible only if it respects
them. Our guarantee is stated on the original free space, with no decomposition.
Learning-based planners. Diffusion models have recently been trained to sample trajectories di-
rectly, for single (Carvalho et al., 2023) and multiple (Shaoul et al., 2025) robots, with constraints
imposed during sampling by projection (Luan et al., 2026) or by receding-horizon denoising (Gia-
retta et al., 2026). These amortize planning into fast learned sampling and represent several routes,
but they enforce feasibility at sampled waypoints at best and produce no optimality certificate. We
compare against them in §4; our contribution is a guarantee rather than a speedup, and the two can
be combined (Appendix A2.2).
Convex relaxations of planning and control. Beyond Graesdal et al. (2026), several works apply
semidefinite or moment-hierarchy relaxations (Shor, 1987; Lasserre, 2001; Parrilo, 2003) to plan-
ning and control problems and mostly report tight solutions empirically, rarely analysing when or
why tightness holds. Teng et al. (2023) formulate kinodynamic motion planning on Lie groups as
polynomial optimization and apply the moment hierarchy; Häring et al. (2024) study minimum-
energy unicycle control with a semidefinite relaxation; Vega et al. (2025) apply the Shor relaxation
to spacecraft manoeuvre planning with an ellipsoidal, probability-of-collision keep-out constraint;
Mahajan et al. (2026) use it for real-time predictive control around ball-shaped obstacles on em-
bedded hardware. Control problems with free timing (Yang et al., 2025; Dong et al., 2026) and
contact-rich planning (Graesdal et al., 2024; Kang et al., 2025) have been relaxed in the same way,
and a sum-of-squares surrogate guides contact-rich search (Wei & Dümbgen, 2026). Outside plan-
ning, such relaxations underlie certifiable estimation, for example registration and SLAM (Yang
et al., 2021; Rosen et al., 2019; Papalia et al., 2024), which we do not review. In contrast to the plan-
ning and control work above, which mostly observes tightness empirically, we prove that a feasible
descent method on the original nonconvex program, started from a fine grid, approaches the optimal
cost of the original program as the class is refined, within any constraint tolerance (Theorem 1).
A1.2
INEXACT SAMPLED CHECKS AND THE DATA FOR FIGURE 1
Sampling and control-polygon tests are not exact.
Sampling t at a finite set is a strict relaxation.
For any finite T = {t1 < ··· < tM} ⊂[0,1], choosing a < b inside any gap between consecutive
samples makes g(t) = (t −a)(t −b) (of degree 2 ≤2d) nonnegative at every sample and negative in
between, with min[0,1] g = −
  b−a
2
2. So the sampled feasible set is in general a proper superset of F
17


at every M and every degree; no finite sample is ever exact. Structurally, each b2d(t0) is an extreme
ray of the dual cone K ∗
2d (12) of the cone (1d) of degree-2d nonnegative polynomials (indexed
by degree, Appendix A3.1.2): if b2d(t0) = x + y with x,y ∈K ∗
2d, pairing with (t −t0)2 forces x’s
representing measure onto {t0}, so a continuum of extreme rays would be replaced by finitely many.
The direction of the error is the unsafe one for a planner: the sampled problem gives a valid lower
bound on the optimal cost of (P) when solved globally, but the curve it returns may penetrate the
obstacle, and the margin violation is only Θ(1/M2): on a uniform grid a gap of width τ = 1/(M−1)
admits violations approaching min[0,1] g = −τ2/4 for g above (for a block the violation scales with
∥g′′∥∞, so Θ(1/M2) holds at bounded speed and acceleration).
Requiring all Bernstein coefficients to be nonnegative is a strict restriction, and degree elevation
does not close it. If g ≥0 vanishes at some t⋆∈(0,1), then since every entry of bD(t⋆) is strictly
positive for every degree D, nonnegative coefficients ζ of g would force ζ⊤bD(t⋆) = 0 with all
terms nonnegative, hence ζ = 0 and g ≡0: no elevation degree ever works for a polynomial with
an interior zero. This case arises here, because whenever a minimizer of (P) touches an obstacle at a
time inside a piece, its avoidance polynomial has an interior double root. The smallest Bernstein-D
coefficient of such a polynomial stays negative and decays only like −t⋆(1 −t⋆)g′′(t⋆)/(2D): for
g(t) = (t −1
2)2 it is exactly −1/
 4(D −1)

for even D and −1/(4D) for odd D, so no elevation is
ever enough. Approaching tangency the required degree likewise diverges: for gε(t) = (t −1
2)2 +ε,
so that min[0,1] gε = ε, the least D with all Bernstein-D coefficients nonnegative satisfies Dmin =
1/(4ε)+O(1); in workspace terms Dmin ≈∥γ ′∥2t⋆(1−t⋆)/(2r jδ) for clearance δ. The avoidance
condition is therefore carried exactly, as written in (1d).
Data for Figure 1.
The figure shows two robots, A (left to right) and B (bottom to top), planned
jointly with one degree-6 Bernstein segment each; each robot is a moving centre for the other (§1).
The obstacles are three ellipses and a circle, each replaced by the smallest scaled ellipse containing
its offset by the robot radius 0.055 (semi-axes raised by 0.055, then by up to 2%), so that the
constraint on the robot’s centre keeps the whole robot clear, and the robots keep their centres at
least 0.11 apart. The cost is the smoothness objective of (P∞) with k = 2 (minimum acceleration),
positions fixed at both ends (ℓ= 0), and no steering constraint. Panel (2) imposes the constraints
only at 8 sample times, ends included; panel (3) runs GlobMP with every constraint on all of [0,1].
The instance has eight route classes (A passes each of the two coloured ellipses on either side, and
either robot crosses first); one opening curve per class, restored and then refined by Algorithm 1,
gives feasible KKT curves in five of them, with costs 1, 1.2, 5.3, 5.6 and 50.8 times the cheapest;
panel (3) shows the cheapest and, in grey, the routes that take a different path in space.
A2
ADDENDUM TO SECTION 2: FORMULATION AND ALGORITHM
A2.1
VARIANTS OF THE FORMULATION
The experiments use four variants of the blocks of (P∞). Each is convex in the position or has a
convex feasible set imposed exactly, so Proposition 10 covers all four. Theorem 1 covers the position
blocks (ellipsoids, robot pairs, boxes and walls); the speed limit constrains γ ′ and lies outside it.
Ellipsoidal obstacles.
A weighted norm ∥x∥Σ = (x⊤Σx)1/2, Σ ⪰0, in the collision constraint,
∥γ(t)−cj(t)∥Σ j ≥r j, keeps every block quadratic; with Σ j ≻0 it describes an ellipsoid.
Several robots.
Several robots are planned jointly by stacking their curves into one γ; let Ea select
robot a’s rows, so γ a = Eaγ. For a robot of radius ra, a sphere of radius r and centre m j is Σ j = E⊤
a Ea,
Eac j = mj, rj = r + ra; two robots a,b are kept apart by Σ j = E⊤
abEab with Eab = Ea −Eb and
rj = ra +rb, so that ∥γ∥Σ j = ∥γ a −γ b∥. The multi-robot experiments of §A4.5 use this separation.
Workspace box and speed limit.
Box constraints use xi −xi ≥0 and xi −xi ≥0 (Appendix A4.6);
a speed limit uses (Tdurvmax)2 −∥γ ′(t)∥2 ≥0, where Tdur is the trajectory duration, so the physical
velocity is γ ′/Tdur. Both are affine or concave quadratic, so their feasible sets are convex.

A2.1.2
REFERENCE MOTIONS FROM LEARNED POLICIES
The reference ρ of (P∞) is a motion supplied by a learned planner or policy (one fixed proposal
per solve), a demonstration or a classical planner. For positions y0,...,yL ∈Rn at normalized times
0 = t0 < ··· < tL = 1, we use the piecewise-linear interpolant
ρ(t) = ti+1 −t
ti+1 −ti
yi + t −ti
ti+1 −ti
yi+1,
t ∈[ti,ti+1],
i = 0,...,L−1.
(7)
It need not have the derivatives required of γ, and it enters the steering constraint (Appendix A2.2).
Relative action commands are first composed from the current configuration into absolute way-
points.
A2.2
STEERING A GENERATIVE PLANNER
Learned planners propose motion as waypoints. Diffusion and flow-matching planners (Janner
et al., 2022; Carvalho et al., 2023; Shaoul et al., 2025; Giaretta et al., 2026) denoise an array
y = (y0,...,yL) of positions at fixed times, one model per map, with start and goal written into
the array at every step. Constraints enter either as gradient guidance, which does not enforce them,
or as a projection of the waypoints, which enforces them at the L+1 sampled times only; Figure 1
and Appendix A1.2 show what happens between samples. We use (P) as that projection, so the
object the sampler carries is a polynomial trajectory whose every block is tested at every t, and the
generator is left untouched.
Given waypoints y, the reference ρ is their piecewise-linear interpolant (7), and the steering con-
straint of (P∞), ∥γ(t)−ρ(t)∥≤¯r for every t, keeps the curve near the proposal (in the Robotarium
runs, a tracking cost to ρ replaces this constraint). Otherwise Algorithms 1–2 run as in §2.
A3
ADDENDUM TO SECTION 3: THEORY AND PROOFS
Appendix A3.1 sets up the chart, the matrices, the compactness facts, Assumption D, and the step-
size and cone-solve bounds (Algorithms 2 and 3). Appendix A3.2 proves the fixed-degree result
(Proposition 10) and Theorem 1, and, under Assumption C stated there, its counterpart at a fixed
number of pieces (Proposition 13) and the degree refinement (Proposition 17); Appendix A3.3 dis-
cusses the assumptions and the stability of the optimal value.

Properties of the cone.
Write Km for the cone (1d) of degree m, so Kj = Kmj, and pζ (t) :=
ζ⊤bm(t) for the polynomial with coefficients ζ . All four properties of Km are immediate from (1d).
It is an intersection of closed homogeneous half-spaces, hence a closed convex cone. It is pointed,
since ζ ,−ζ ∈Km forces the polynomial to vanish, hence ζ = 0 as bm is a basis. It has nonempty
interior, since bm is a nonnegative partition of unity, so |pζ (t)| ≤∥ζ ∥∞≤∥ζ ∥and any ζ with
min[0,1] pζ = µ > 0 carries the open µ-ball. And it is semialgebraic, by Tarski–Seidenberg applied
to ∀t (0 ≤t ≤1 →pζ (t) ≥0).
The dual cone, the set of vectors with nonnegative inner product with every element of Km, is
K ∗
m = cone{bm(t) : t ∈[0,1]},
(12)
the nonnegative combinations of the evaluation vectors used in §2. This holds because Km is by
definition the dual of that conic hull, the hull is closed (each element needs at most m+1 generators
by Carathéodory, and u⊤bm(t) ≡1 bounds the total weight, so the generating data lie in a compact
set), and the bipolar theorem (a closed convex cone is the dual of its own dual) applies.
Lipschitz bound for the restoration merit.
For general C2 coefficient maps on a compact convex
region R, set e j(z) = π j(z)−PKj(π j(z)). Projection residuals are nonexpansive and 0 ∈Kj, hence,
with Bπ,j and BJ,j bounds on ∥π j∥and ∥Dπ j∥op over R and LJ,j a Lipschitz constant of Dπ j
(computed below for spherical exclusions), ∥e j(z)∥≤Bπ,j and ∥ej(z)−ej(y)∥≤BJ,j∥z−y∥. The
gradient 2Dπ j(z)⊤e j(z) therefore has Lipschitz constant at most 2B2
J,j + 2LJ,jBπ,j, and summing
over blocks bounds the full merit; the step scale of Appendix A3.1.4 uses the larger, also valid,
constant with 4B2
J,j. The remaining calculation specializes to spherical position exclusions (Σ j = I).

Compactness and existence on each component.
Lemma 6 gives the compact sublevel sets used
in the proof of Proposition 10. Lemmas 7 and 8 are not needed for the main results; they record that
the feasible set splits into finitely many connected components, each with its own cheapest curve,
which is why GlobMP is run from several seeds in §4.

Lemma 7. Suppose every coefficient map is continuous and semialgebraic, that is, described by
finitely many polynomial equations and inequalities (polynomial maps qualify). Consider the set of
feasible curves, Abc ∩T
j π −1
j (Kj), identified with F through the chart of Lemma 3. Then:
(i) Finitely many pieces. F splits into finitely many separate pieces F1,...,F¯c, its connected
components: two feasible curves lie in the same piece exactly when one can be deformed
continuously into the other without leaving F.
(ii) Each piece is closed. Each Fc is closed in Rn×(d+1), and also open relative to F (clopen
in F).
(iii) The pieces are a positive distance apart.
If U is a nonempty compact subset of one
piece Fc, then every feasible curve outside that piece stays at distance at least d0(U ) :=
dist(U ,F \Fc) > 0 from U ; we set d0(U ) = +∞when F has only the one piece.
Proof of Lemma 7. F = Abc ∩T
j π −1
j (Kj) is closed, as a finite intersection of preimages of closed
sets under continuous maps. It is semialgebraic: each π j and each Kj is (the quantifier “for every
t ∈[0,1]” is eliminated by the Tarski–Seidenberg theorem), and preimages and finite intersections
preserve this (Bochnak et al., 1998).
(i) A semialgebraic set has finitely many connected components, each path connected (Bochnak
et al., 1998).
(ii) A connected component is closed in its set, hence in Rn×(d+1) since F is closed. Its complement
in F is a finite union of closed components, so Fc is also open in F.
(iii) W := F \Fc is closed by (ii) and disjoint from U . If W̸ = /0, then x 7→dist(x,W ) is continuous
and positive on the compact set U , so its minimum d0(U ) is positive.

of the feasible set contains a cheapest curve: there is a Γ⋆

Cumulative constraints and derivative-cost curvature.
The finite-dimensional reduction of (P∞)
also admits scalar budget blocks. Write z = vec(Γfree) and let π j(z) denote a budget block (1e). Its
cone is R+, so its contribution to the restoration merit and gradient is
Vj(z) = min{πj(z),0}2,
∇Vj(z) = 2min{πj(z),0}∇π j(z).
These formulas do not require the integrand to be polynomial in time. For a general integrand,
differentiation under the integral is justified, for example, if it is measurable in time and, for almost
every t, C2 in the trajectory arguments, with its value and first two composed z-derivatives dominated
by integrable functions on each compact set of free coordinates.
For a single segment of degree d ≥k with only start and goal fixed, the objective of (P∞) has H ≻0
if and only if k ≤2 (the case ℓ= 0 of Assumption B): a free perturbation has zero derivative cost
exactly when its degree is below k, and among those vanishing at t = 0 and t = 1 the first nonzero
one is t(1 −t), of degree 2. When the cost has free zero-curvature directions, compactness must
be checked separately. Additional endpoint derivatives can remove these directions as in Lemma 9;
adding them changes the boundary data of (P∞).
A3.1.3
THE STRUCTURAL ASSUMPTION
Assumption D (finite-dimensional representation). d ≥k and 2(ℓ+ 1) ≤d, and every pointwise
coefficient map and scalar cumulative map is C2 on an open neighbourhood of the regions used in
the analysis.
The quadratic obstacle and separation blocks, and budget blocks whose integrand meets the condi-
tion of Appendix A3.1.2, satisfy the smoothness part.
The degree condition makes A of full column rank, since its columns are independent Hermite
interpolation conditions (Stoer & Bulirsch, 2002); N is an orthonormal basis of kerA⊤, and Q ⪰0
always. Under Assumption D, Assumption B is equivalent to H ≻0 for the derivative objective,
with H as in Lemma 4 (Lemmas 5 and 9).
Write Pr for scalar polynomials of degree at most r, with Pr = {0} for r < 0, and coeff(·) for the
Bernstein-d coefficient vector of a polynomial.
Lemma 9 (kernels). Let 0 ≤k ≤d, let A be the endpoint matrix of (9), and read each coefficient
vector as the polynomial of degree at most d it represents. The vectors with zero kth-derivative cost
are exactly the polynomials of degree below k, and the vectors with zero endpoint data are exactly
the multiples of [t(1−t)]ℓ+1:
kerG(k) = coeff(Pk−1),

Under Assumption D, the second space has dimension df = d +1−2(ℓ+1).
Proof of Lemma 9. Let pζ be the polynomial with coefficients ζ . Since ζ ⊤G(k)ζ =

where BJ,j = maxR ∥Dπ j∥op, Bπ,j = maxR ∥π j∥, and LJ,j is the Lipschitz constant of the Jacobian

for 0 < ηproj ≤1/Lproj. The step must be kept in the region, which compactness does not do by itself,
and constants taken over a ball around the unknown minimizer are not computable. Algorithm 2
keeps every nonzero residual, so omitted blocks have zero gradient and every update is a step on the
full merit V(z) = ∑j dist2(π j(z),Kj), the merit (2) in the coordinates z. With exact cone projections
and the region condition above, telescoping Tproj executed steps gives

The bound covers scalar cumulative blocks and does not need H ≻0. It gives a small merit gradient,
not feasibility: for the scalar block π(z) = z2 −1 ∈R+ of one coordinate z, V(z) = (1 −z2)2 near
zero and V ′(0) = 0 although V(0) = 1.
The cone solve and its accuracy.
Algorithm 3 approximates the projection onto Km for any de-
gree m ≥0, with m = mj for block j. By Moreau’s identity it projects −π/∥π∥onto the dual cone
K ∗
m with the atoms bm(t) and adds ∥π∥times the result to π; Tcone is a numerical budget.
The normalized correlation gives the best decrease along a single nonnegative ray.
It is not
a polynomial in t: if µ(t) = ⟨v,bm(t)⟩and ∥bm(t)∥2 > 0, its interior stationary points satisfy
2µ′∥bm∥2 −µ(∥bm∥2)′ = 0. Evaluate the correlation at these roots and at the endpoints; an identi-
cally zero derivative means the correlation is constant.
A direct rate argument specializes the conic pursuit framework of Locatello et al. (2017). Let ω ν be
an exact fully corrective iterate, ω ∗= PK ∗
m (y) (so ω ⋆= ∥π∥ω ∗in (3)), and measure the gap
θν = 1
2∥y−ω ν∥2 −1
2∥y−ω ∗∥2 ≥0,
which is nonnegative because ω ∗is the nearest point of the cone.
For the normalized atoms at = bm(t)/∥bm(t)∥, nonnegative entries summing to one give ∥bm(t)∥≤
1, so u⊤at ≥1 for the vector of ones u. Since ∥ω ∗∥≤∥y∥= 1, it has an atomic representation of
total weight at most √m+1.
Exact nonnegative least squares gives ⟨y −ω ν,ω ν⟩= 0. Convexity then bounds θν by √m+1
times the largest positive atom correlation. Adding that unit atom with its optimal nonnegative
weight decreases the objective by half the squared correlation; full correction does at least as well.

If the correlation test stops the loop at iteration ν, then θν−1 ≤√m+1ε. Strong convexity of the

Its polynomial values are at least −∥π∥ε, since that vector is −∥π∥v. For ε > 0 these bound the error
rather than give exact membership, and stopping on the budget gives neither bound. Proposition 10
and Theorem 1 assume an exact solve of the convex problem (5), which is not this cone projection;
Algorithm 3 plays no role there.
A3.2
PROOF OF THEOREM 1
Throughout this subsection, the blocks are polynomial in Γ, as the sphere, ellipsoid, pair and affine
blocks, and budgets with polynomial integrands, are, and z stands for Γfree. It uses the boundary
chart (Lemma 3) and the compact cost sublevel sets (Lemma 6) of Appendix A3.1; the push maps
that move a curve off the obstacles (Lemma 14) serve only Appendix A3.2.3 and the stability results
of Appendix A3.3.
A3.2.1
FIXED DEGREE
Let E⋆denote the optimum of (P). Its collision blocks (j ≤p) are polynomial and convex in the
position, and its steering block, speed limit and (assumed convex) budgets have convex feasible sets,
all imposed through D.
GlobMP starts from the cheapest point of a grid. Take a box B with a global minimizer of (P) in its
interior, and let
zh ∈argmin

f(z) : z ∈F ∩hZnd f ∩B
	
,
in the rational chart of free control points described after the grid part below. The box may be the
whole chart: f has compact sublevel sets with finitely many grid points, so a cheapest feasible grid
point exists whenever one grid point is feasible. The start of Theorem 1 and Proposition 13 is this
point for the class SN, with B the whole chart and the blocks of the problem being solved; there df
counts the free control points per coordinate left by the boundary and junction equations.
Proposition 13 rests on the following result at fixed degree, which uses a constraint qualification
on the control points. Theorem 1 uses only its inner-set and descent steps, which do not need
Assumption E.
Assumption E (constraint qualification). For some U > E⋆, every z ∈F with f(z) ≤U has a v
with π(z)+Dπ(z)v ∈intK .
Assumption E is Robinson’s condition (Robinson, 1976): some change of the control points makes
every block strictly feasible to first order. It is needed only on this sublevel set, and on fine splines it
follows from Assumption C (Appendix A3.2.3, Lemma 16).
Proposition 10 (GlobMP at fixed degree). Assume Assumptions D and B (so that H ≻0) and As-
sumption E, and use a step 0 < η < 2/β. Run the repair (5) from a feasible start of cost at most U.
Then every iterate is feasible, each step lowers the cost by at least (1/η −β/2)∥zν+1 −zν∥2, and
every limit point of the iterates is a KKT point of (P) (defined in the grid part of the proof). If the
start is the cheapest feasible grid point zh and the grid spacing h is small enough, then moreover the
costs decrease to the optimum, f(zν) ↓E⋆, and every limit point is a global minimizer of (P).
The proof has a descent part and a grid part. With uniform feasible random starts, a run with globally
minimizing limit points is obtained with probability one after finitely many starts (Corollary 11).
Proof of Proposition 10, descent part.

Proof of Proposition 10, grid part.
(i) Let z⋆be a global minimizer in the interior of B and v the

and every limit point of zh is feasible with cost E⋆.
(ii) The extended cost ϕ := f on F, +∞elsewhere, is lower semicontinuous, semialgebraic and
continuous on its closed domain (π is polynomial; the quantifier over t is eliminated by Tarski–
Seidenberg). So its Clarke critical points take finitely many values (Bolte et al., 2007, Cor. 9(ii)). A
KKT point of (P) is a feasible z with

and, by (25), take h0 with f(zh) < E⋆+ ε0 for h ≤h0. By the descent part, the costs decrease to a
limit ¯E attained at KKT points, with
E⋆≤¯E ≤f(zh) < E⋆+ε0;
¯E is a critical value, so ¯E = E⋆, and every limit point is a global minimizer. The iterates are bounded,
so they approach the compact set of global minimizers.
The chart of Lemma 3 has irrational entries in general. The control points that the boundary condi-
tions (for a spline, also the junction conditions) leave free form a rational affine chart of the curve
class: these conditions fix the others in terms of these and the data (for one segment, the data alone).
For a single segment, a move of the free coordinates moves Γ by the same amount in the Frobe-
nius norm and the free coordinates have norm at most ∥Γ∥F, which is all Proposition 17 uses of
Lemma 3; for a spline class such as SN the chart is still an affine bijection, which is all the grid part
of Proposition 10 needs. In both cases the membership test is exact in rational arithmetic for rational
data and breakpoints, by the Sturm-sequence test stated before Proposition 17.
26


Corollary 11 (random starts). Assume the hypotheses of Proposition 10, with B bounded. Draw
starting points independently and uniformly at random from F ∩B, run the exact repair (5) from
each (each run understood in its infinite-iteration limit), and keep the cheapest limiting cost. There
is a number ϑ > 0, depending on the instance, such that after ns starts the kept cost is above E⋆
with probability at most (1−ϑ)ns. So, with probability one, some run reaches globally minimizing
limit points after finitely many starts.
Proof of Corollary 11.
Part (i) of the grid part gives a ball in F ∩B on which f < E⋆+ ε0; let
ϑ ∈(0,1] be the ratio of its volume to that of F ∩B. A start in the ball is feasible with cost below
E⋆+ε0, which is all part (ii) uses, so its run reaches only global minimizers. By independence,
Pr{none of the first ns starts lies in the ball} = (1−ϑ)ns →0.
Uniform starts are drawn by rejection from B, since membership in F is decided exactly (rounding
each draw to rationals finely enough keeps a positive probability of landing in the ball). The grid
of Proposition 10, in contrast, has O((diamB/h)nd f ) points: that proposition establishes global
convergence, not an efficient search.

the gap) ≥ra +rb +2˜r′ stays > ra +rb +2˜r; a reference distance ≤¯r−˜r′ stays < ¯r−˜r; a wall offset
≥˜r′ stays > ˜r. An ellipsoid norm enlarges the move by at most λmax(Σ j)1/2. The interpolant is Ck−1
of degree 2k −1 ≤d and matches γ to order k −1 at the endpoints, so for ℓ< k it lies in SN.

of Proposition 17), hence equicontinuous; with the endpoint clearance σ/2, there is τ0 ∈(0,1/4],

A3.2.3
GLOBAL MINIMIZERS OVER SN UNDER A CONDITION ON CONFIGURATIONS
Theorem 1 does not say that GlobMP reaches a global minimizer over a fixed class SN. It does for
(P∞) and large N under the following condition.
Assumption C (free configurations). With g j as in §2, at every time and feasible position, some v
increases every active constraint: ∇xg j(x,t)⊤v > 0 whenever gj(x,t) = 0.
Assumption C is the Mangasarian–Fromovitz condition on configurations; it rules out jamming and
is used only at feasible positions within distance 1 of the points visited by curves of cost at most
E⋆
∞+ 1 (Lemma 14 and the proof of Proposition 13). Under it, GlobMP from zh reaches global
minimizers over every fine enough class SN; the proof chains four results and is at the end of this
subsection.
Proposition 13 (global minimizers over SN). Under Assumptions A, B and C:
(i) There is N0 such that, for every N ≥N0, GlobMP over SN on (P∞) with the repair (5), a step
0 < η < 2/β (β computed for SN) and the start zh with h small enough produces curves feasible
at every instant, with nonincreasing cost, and all its limit points are global minimizers over SN.
(ii) For N ≥N0, the optimal cost E⋆
N over SN satisfies E⋆

independent of N, and as N →∞subsequences of these minimizers converge uniformly to global
minimizers of (P∞).

Lemma 14 (pushing into the interior). Fix a time and let the g j be C1 functions of the position, so
that X := {x ∈Rn : g j(x) ≥0 ∀j} is the set of feasible positions. Assume Assumption C at this time:
at every x ∈X some direction v increases all active constraints, ∇gj(x)·v > 0 whenever gj(x) = 0.
Assume also that the start and goal positions are strictly feasible. Then for every compact Q ⊂Rn
there are numbers µ0,G > 0 and, for each 0 < µ ≤µ0, a smooth map Φµ : Rn →Rn, a push, such

x ∈X ∩Q;

µ;

Lemma 16 (Assumption C gives Assumption E). Under the hypotheses of Lemma 15, fix any U.
For all large enough N, every feasible curve of SN with cost at most U satisfies Robinson’s con-
dition: some change v of its control points makes every block strictly feasible to first order, as in
Assumption E.
Proof of Lemma 16.
Let φ (x,t) be the space-time field of the proof of Lemma 15, on a compact
set containing every curve of cost ≤U (bounded by coercivity, Proposition 17); it is Ck, vanishes
near the endpoints, and ∇xgj · φ ≥2 where g j < κ. Let γ be feasible in SN with cost ≤U, and
v the Hermite interpolant of t 7→φ (γ(t),t). By equicontinuity, for large N the end pieces of γ
lie where φ = 0, so v has zero boundary data and is an admissible change of control points. As
∥(φ ◦γ)(k)∥L2 ≤c4(U), (28) gives
∥v−φ ◦γ∥∞≤c4N−(k−1/2) < 1/G for large N,
(40)
so ∇xgj ·v ≥1 where gj < κ. Hence, for small s > 0, the piecewise polynomial gj +s∇xgj ·v, with
coefficients π j + Dπ j(sv), is ≥gj + s > 0 where gj < κ and ≥κ −sGsup∥v∥> 0 elsewhere: its
coefficients lie in intKj, which is Assumption E with sv.
The degree-refinement procedure.
At level r, take degrees dr ↑∞satisfying Assumption D, and

the grid spacing and a box of free control points (in the rational chart after the proof of Proposi-
tion 10) centred at zero.
Degree elevation keeps the previous output without changing its curve or cost. Algorithm 1 may
stop after finitely many iterations, since a trial replaces its seed only when it is feasible and no more
expensive.
Feasibility of each grid candidate and of each trial is tested exactly over [0,1], piece by piece,
for rational piecewise-polynomial data and breakpoints. For a trial, its free control points in the
rational chart are first rounded to rationals, and this rounded curve is the one kept. On each piece,
Sturm sequences isolate the roots in the open interval of the piece within disjoint closed rational
subintervals of it, with no root at their endpoints. The sign of the block is then checked at one
rational point between consecutive isolating intervals, and between each end of the piece and its
nearest interval (or at one interior rational point if there is no root). A piece on which the block
vanishes identically passes.

Proof of Proposition 17.
We use two properties, proved at the end: coercivity (curves with the
boundary data and bounded cost are bounded in H k) and the approach from inside (for every ε > 0
some C∞curve with the boundary data costs at most E⋆
∞+ε and has every constraint bounded below
by a positive number). The constraints gj need only depend continuously on t and on derivatives of
order below k.
Existence: a minimizing sequence is bounded, so a subsequence converges weakly in H k and
uniformly with the derivatives below k; the limit keeps the data and constraints, and E is weakly
lower semicontinuous.
Approximation: fix ε and γ ε from the approach from inside, and δ > 0 such that every curve with
the boundary data within 2δ of γ ε in Ck is feasible with cost ≤E⋆
∞+2ε. The Bernstein polynomials
of γ ε converge in Cmax{k,ℓ} (Lorentz, 1986); correcting their boundary data by a Hermite polynomial
of degree 2ℓ+1 gives γ d with the data and ∥γ d −γ ε∥Ck ≤δ for d ≥d(ε). For a degree-d curve with
control points Γ,

Coercivity: if ∥γ i∥H k →∞with bounded cost, the normalized curves have kth derivatives and
boundary data tending to zero; by the compact embedding H k ⊂H k−1, a subsequence converges
in H k−1, hence (the kth derivatives tending to zero) in H k, to a polynomial of degree below k and
norm one whose derivatives of orders 0,...,min{ℓ,k−1} vanish at both ends. Under Assumption B
it is zero (proof of Lemma 5), a contradiction.

(i) Proposition 10 over SN, with B the whole chart, gives limit points that are global minimizers
over SN; Corollary 11 gives the same for independent uniform feasible starts in a bounded box
containing a global minimizer in its interior.

A3.3
SCOPE OF THE ASSUMPTIONS AND STABILITY
This subsection records which parts of Assumptions A and B the proofs need and which can be
weakened, when the optimal value is stable, and when Assumption C, used only in Propositions 13
and 17 and in the linear stability bound below, holds and why Proposition 13 cannot drop it. Theo-
rem 1 needs only Assumptions A and B.
Piecewise-polynomial data.
Assumption A asks for continuous, piecewise-polynomial obstacle
centres and reference, which covers the piecewise-linear reference (7) of a learned policy. The exact
test and the critical-value step of the proof of Proposition 10 use polynomial data.
The cone test stays exact for rational data and breakpoints: on each interval of a common partition
of [0,1] into the pieces of the curve and of the data, the block is a polynomial in t, and Kj becomes
the product of the cones of these intervals, still closed and convex. The problem over SN stays
semialgebraic, which is all that step needs.
The push, the rate and Lemma 16 use only continuity of the centres and the reference: the field φ of
Lemma 14 is a smooth partition of unity times fixed vectors, and continuity of g j and ∇xgj in (x,t)
is all its construction needs. Beyond the exact test, the proof of Theorem 1 uses only continuity of
the data.
Bounded curves instead of Assumption B.
Assumption B enters only through boundedness:
curves of bounded cost are bounded. The descent step uses the upper curvature β and not α. When
the steering constraint is present (¯r < ∞), every feasible curve has ∥γ∥∞≤∥ρ∥∞+ ¯r, and the co-
ercivity argument of Proposition 17 applies unchanged: a normalized sequence with vanishing kth
derivatives and vanishing sup norm cannot converge to a polynomial of norm one. So the conclu-
sions of Theorem 1 and Proposition 13 hold with ¯r < ∞in place of Assumption B, with f convex
rather than strongly convex over SN and compactness supplied by the bounded feasible set.
Stability of the optimal value.
E⋆
∞(˜r) is nondecreasing in ˜r and left-continuous on (−˜rmax, ˜rmax]:
if ˜ri ↑˜r, minimizers of (P˜ri∞) cost at most E⋆
∞(˜r), and, as in the proof of Theorem 1(iii), a subsequence
converges to a curve feasible for (P˜r
∞) of cost at most limi E⋆
∞(˜ri) (or E⋆
∞(˜ri) →∞if (P˜r
∞) is infeasible).
Hence E⋆
∞has at most countably many jumps, all upward on the right. Let
Erob := lim
˜r↓0 E⋆
∞(˜r).
(42)
By compactness, a curve along which every constraint holds strictly keeps a positive margin, so
every method whose curves keep a positive clearance costs at least Erob, the analogue of the robust
33


optimum of Solovey et al. (2020). We call (P∞) stable if Erob = E⋆
∞; with ˜r in place of 0, this holds
at all but countably many ˜r.

∞(˜r) ≤E⋆
∞+c ˜r
(small ˜r > 0),
(43)
within (c+1)δN of E⋆
∞. Bound (43) holds if Assumption C holds along one optimal curve (an open
condition): the construction of Lemma 14 near that curve, used as in the proof of Lemma 15, gives a
curve with every gj ≥ρ and cost at most E⋆
∞+c2ρ, and g j ≥ρ is a margin of at least ρ/(4G′) for a
constant G′ of the data. Theorem 1 then recovers the rate of Proposition 13(ii) with exactly feasible
curves.
When Assumption C holds.
It is needed only at feasible positions within distance 1 of curves of
cost at most E⋆
∞+ 1. It always holds for robots without obstacles, walls or steering: the direction
v = x, which spreads every robot away from the origin, gives
∇gj ·v = 2∥xa −xb∥2 > 0
for every touching pair. Spreading about the centre of a single ball obstacle does the same for that
obstacle and any number of robots.
For static constraints it holds for almost every choice of margins. Give block j the radius rj +δj. For
each set J of blocks, the map x 7→(gj(x))j∈J is polynomial, so by Sard’s theorem almost every
value is regular. At every configuration where exactly the blocks of J are active, their gradients
are then linearly independent, which implies Assumption C. There are finitely many sets J , so the
exceptional margins δ form a null set. For moving obstacles or a moving reference the same count
suggests that, generically, the condition fails only at isolated points of space-time, and a cheapest
curve may pass through one.
Proposition 13 cannot drop Assumption C.
The condition is needed by the algorithm, not only
by the proof. Take a point robot in R2, two discs of radius r centred at (±r,0), which touch at the
origin, where Assumption C fails, and the cost cx2 with c > 0 over x2 ≥−1 (adding (ε/2)∥x∥2,
ε > 0, changes nothing below). The vertical line x1 = 0 is feasible throughout. From (0,y) with
0 < y ≤ηc, the linearized set is
{w : w2 ≥y/2+(r/y)|w1|},
and the repair returns its apex (0,y/2). So the iterates halve their distance to the origin at every step
and converge to it. The origin is not a KKT point, since the cost still decreases along the feasible
line. A cheapest curve through a zero-width gap between two touching obstacles can stall GlobMP
in the same way.
A4
ADDENDUM TO SECTION 4: EXPERIMENTS
A4.1
EXPERIMENTAL SETUP
This subsection summarizes the hardware, robot platforms and software shared across all experi-
ments. GlobMP’s runs below (D3IL aside, Appendix A4.4) are instances of (P), each built from
blocks of (P∞), Appendix A2.1 or the maze keep-outs (44) of Appendix A4.3 and solved by Algo-
rithms 1–2 under the protocol of its source. All reported baseline results are obtained from our own
reproductions.
Hardware.
The D3IL and maze baselines ran on a workstation with a 24-core Intel Core Ultra 9
285K at 3.7 GHz and one NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5090 (32 GB); the synthetic study of §4.1 and
the multi-robot suite of §A4.5 ran on a shared node with two 32-core AMD EPYC 7502 CPUs and
eight NVIDIA A100-PCIE GPUs (40 GB each), as did GlobMP’s maze runs, so the maze times
compare different machines. Training, sampling and model evaluation were performed on a single
GPU. The RRT, GCS and SDP-MP baselines ran on CPU with each run pinned to fixed cores, and
Algorithms 1–2 ran on CPU or GPU as stated with each experiment (for the synthetic study, in
Appendix A4.2).

(a) Robot Platform

Fingertip tactile
Figure 9: Robot platform and operator station. The operator’s finger joints come from Manus gloves
and the wrist poses from VIVE trackers against two base stations; retargeting turns both into absolute
joint targets for the 38 arm and hand joints of a Dexmate Vega-1 carrying two XHAND1 hands, sent
at 30 Hz. Head and wrist cameras and the fingertip force sensors record alongside. The close-up is
the same hand photographed on the bench and cut out of its background; the five fingertip pads, 120
taxels each, are tinted amber and the leader points at one of them; they are the only tactile surfaces
the hand has, the thumb’s included. The palm and the finger links sense nothing. Only the resulting
trajectories are used in this paper; none of the operator hardware is present at inference.
Robot platforms.
The bimanual demonstrations are collected on the setup of Figure 9, using a
Dexmate Vega-1 with two XHAND1 dexterous hands. Two ZED X One S cameras are mounted
at the wrists and one ZED X Mini camera is mounted on the head. Teleoperation uses Manus
gloves for hand target gestures and HTC VIVE trackers for wrist target poses. Only the resulting
trajectories enter this paper; none of the teleoperation hardware is used at inference. Figure 10 shows
the modalities, the task string, the camera images and the fingertip tactile arrays, at the instant the
tissue box changes hands in the handover task.
The Robotarium experiments of §4.2 use the Georgia Tech Robotarium (Wilson et al., 2020),
with its 3.2 × 2.0 m arena, GRITSBot geometry, and differential-drive dynamics. The benchmark
environments of §4.2 are adapted to the Robotarium workspace, with obstacles scaled by 0.85
(Cross-Room), 0.4 (Drop-Region), 0.2 (Corridor-Bay, eight posts) and 1.1 (Conveyor,
with wider channels and a model retrained for them), represented virtually in the arena. The GRITS-
Bots have physical radius 0.055 m, while planning uses an inflated radius r = 0.13 m to account for
tracking error, the testbed’s barrier-certificate safety layer and the non-holonomic conversion of the
plan. The planned trajectories are tracked through the Robotarium’s single-integrator-to-unicycle
controller, and an overhead camera records the executed motion. The virtual obstacles are over-
laid from the corresponding map definitions, and clearance is evaluated from the robot trajectories
tracked in the video after correcting for lens distortion. Across the four obstacle maps, no GlobMP
robot comes closer than 0.06 m to an obstacle; the closest pass occurs on Conveyor and is shown
in Figure 11; Figures 18–22, at the end of the appendix, show every method except PCD-LB (at zero
in Figure 5) on each map.
Software.
All experiments are based on PyTorch (2.13; 2.6 for Maze2D) for automatic differentia-
tion, JAX 0.6.2 for DiRecT’s SCP–ADMM projector, and IPOPT through Pyomo for the augmented-
Lagrangian projection, under an external wall-clock cap. The RRT variants run through OMPL (¸Su-
can et al., 2012); IRIS and GCS through Drake (Tedrake & the Drake Development Team, 2019);
and the curve fits, SDP-MP and GlobMP’s SQP polish through CVXPY (Diamond & Boyd, 2016)
with Clarabel (Goulart & Chen, 2024). The multi-robot baselines build on MMD (Shaoul et al.,
2025) (MIT), MDOC (He et al., 2026) (MIT) and DiRecT (Giaretta et al., 2026) (MIT), itself an ex-
tension of PROJECTED COUPLED DIFFUSION (Luan et al., 2026) (MIT). Every learned multi-robot,
maze and D3IL baseline is trained from scratch under its source’s protocol rather than scored from
a released checkpoint.

A4.2
SYNTHETIC EXPERIMENTS
The synthetic study of §4.1 tests continuous-time feasibility, the cost of the returned curve, and
scalability, on Problem 1 and a variant of it. Every instance is a minimum-derivative problem with
snap cost k = 4, endpoint data ℓ= 4, piecewise degree-9 polynomials, C3 junctions and margin
0.005 m (added once to pair separations), so the piecewise conditions of Appendix A4.6 apply and
the planning curve lies in the H 4 class of (P∞).
Problem 1 (robots exchanging places among spheres). Na robots, Na even, are stacked into one
curve γ with values in R2Na and form Na/2 exchanging pairs: in pair i one robot moves from (x0,yi)
to (x0 +Di,yi) and the other the reverse way along yi +oi, with Di uniform on [6,8] m and oi uniform
on [0.3,0.9]RS with RS = 1.2 m, consecutive pairs separated by 2.8 to 3.6RS (from the upper row
of one to the lower row of the next). Every pair of robots carries a separation block (Σ j = E⊤
abEab,
cj = 0, rj = RS; Appendix A2.1), and every robot a ball block for each static sphere, with Eac j its
centre, Σ j = E⊤
a Ea and r j its radius. Two spheres of radius uniform on [0.45,0.65] m sit 0.3 to 0.9
radii below the first row, so the first robot’s straight path enters both, and every start and goal clears
them by 0.2 m. There is no workspace bound, so every inequality block has a nonconvex feasible set.
Each exchanging pair, and each robot whose straight path enters or passes within 5 cm of a sphere,
for that sphere, is passed on one of two sides, which gives 8 to 128 route classes per instance.
36


GRITSbot, r = 5.5 cm
virtual obstacle
start ○  goal □
closest pass:
6 cm clear
Figure 11: GlobMP on the Georgia Tech Robotarium: twelve GRITSBots on Conveyor in the
3.2 × 2.0 m arena, at t = 25 s. The paths the robots drove, starts (circles), goals (squares), and the
virtual obstacles drawn from the map. The circle magnifies the run’s closest pass: 6 cm between the
robot’s body and a belt.
The main comparison uses N = 7 pieces and 20 instances at each of Na = 2,4,6,8. The scaling
study uses N = 4 pieces, lower rows of consecutive pairs spaced 3RS apart with oi = 0.6RS and one
Di shared by all pairs, three spheres, and, from 2 to 200 robots, 10 seeds per size for GlobMP and
5 for the baselines. GlobMP’s scaling runs start from the straight-line curve (Figure 3 reports only
this stage; no route classes, polish or finishing step enter it) and are timed from process start to the
first feasible curve, setup included, on CPU (Figure 3a) and separately on one A100 (28 s median at
200 robots). Blocks are counted per piece: at 200 robots, 19,900 pair and 600 robot-sphere blocks
over 4 pieces give the 82,000 blocks of §4.1 and Figure 3c.
Methods.
In the main comparison, GlobMP opens each route class with a waypoint path that
passes every pair and every crossed sphere on the chosen side, runs Algorithms 1–2 (then an SQP
polish and the finishing step of Appendix A4.6), and returns the cheapest feasible result over the
classes. RRT-Connect and RRT∗are the OMPL implementations (Kuffner & LaValle, 2000; Kara-
man & Frazzoli, 2011) in R2Na with the exact validity test (every pair at least RS apart, every robot
outside every sphere); they sample in the rectangle spanned by the instance plus 3 m, since a sam-
pling planner needs a bounded region, and get a 60 s budget (600 s in the scaling study). Their path
is collision-free as a polyline but is not a curve of the class, so the returned curve is the cheapest
collision-free fit among several (else the least colliding) on the true geometry (dense check, 0.1 mm
tolerance): the minimum-snap interpolant through the path at the segment breakpoints, which cuts
corners; fits that trade snap against the squared distance to the path; and minimum-snap fits kept
within a corridor around the path wherever the current fit collides, with the corridor width set by
the path’s own clearance (Mellinger & Kumar, 2011; Richter et al., 2016), each on the path and on
a shortcut version of it. GCS (Marcucci et al., 2023) works in the same joint space, where both
collision sets are convex: robot a inside a sphere is {x : ∥xa −Eacj∥≤rj} and a colliding pair is
{x : ∥xa −xb∥≤RS}. IRIS (Deits & Tedrake, 2015a) grows regions from seeds every 0.5 m along
a 60 s RRT-Connect path (GCS budget 2400 s, 600 s in the scaling study), Drake’s GCS trajectory
optimization finds the minimum path-energy path through them (plus a 10−3 time cost) with Bézier
order 5, falling back to 3 and 2 where its convex solve fails numerically, and its curve is fitted in the
same way. SDP-MP, the semidefinite relaxation of Graesdal et al. (2026) extended to pair blocks, is
written on the same program in its own affine chart, so that the endpoint and junction conditions hold
by construction; the per-segment form with lifted junctions stops with a numerical error in Clarabel
from two segments on. The chart form is joint over all robots and segments, and each block must lie
on each segment in the cone of polynomials nonnegative on [0,1] of §2, written as sums of squares.
On an obstacle-free instance it reproduces the exact minimum-snap optimum. Its rounded curve is
37


the first-order part of the solution. The baselines run on CPU, one run per four pinned cores, and a
run that returns nothing within one hour counts as a failure. GlobMP’s route classes run on CPU in
the same way at Na = 2,4 and on the first 10 instances at Na = 6, and on A100 GPUs, four to ten runs
per GPU, for the rest, so its times are totals of work rather than a like-for-like speed comparison.
Rate in the number of pieces.
Figure 4 uses instance 0 of the main comparison with Na = 2,
degree 9, Ck−1 joins and the 0.5 cm margin, for k ∈{1,2,3,4} and ℓ∈{k −1,k}, and N up to 12

the kth-derivative coefficients, with the cost quadratic expanded around the obstacle-free minimizer

A4.3
MAZE NAVIGATION
We examine whether the proposed formulation remains effective in a nonconvex maze. We consider
the safe maze navigation benchmark of Xiao et al. (2025), following the setting reported by Giaretta
et al. (2026). A point robot traverses the D4RL maze2d-large environment (Fu et al., 2020)
between fixed endpoints while avoiding keep-out regions placed within the maze corridors. The
state contains planar position and velocity, and the action is a planar force. A rollout is considered
safe if the executed trajectory remains outside every keep-out region. We evaluate two settings,
Broad, containing two keep-outs, and Narrow, containing six.
This benchmark combines both constraint constructions used in this paper. The maze walls form
a union of cells and therefore define a disjunctive free space that cannot be represented by a sin-
gle membership in K . A breadth-first search on the cell graph selects a corridor in ≈1 ms, after
which the continuous trajectory is optimized and verified within the selected corridor. Each of the
12 corridor boxes along the route contributes four affine blocks (6) and one Bézier segment, with
adjacent segments joined Cℓ, resulting in 48 affine blocks. Each keep-out contributes one block (44),
quadratic for e = 2 and quartic for e = 4, beyond the quadratic blocks of (P∞). We use k = ℓ= 2
with pieces of degree 9, so Corollary 2(i) (ℓ< k) does not apply here. The maze geometry is not
learned. The baselines rely on the diffusion prior to remain within the maze and explicitly constrain
only the keep-outs among the geometric constraints, whereas our formulation enforces the corridor
boundaries through affine blocks and does not use a learned prior.
Baselines. We compare against ten diffusion planners, nine of them constrained: Diffuser (Janner
et al., 2022), Classifier Guidance (Dhariwal & Nichol, 2021), Gradient Guidance (Chung et al.,
2023), Projected Diffusion (Christopher et al., 2024), Primal–Dual and Augmented Lagrangian
(Zhang et al., 2025), the SafeDiffuser variants RoS, ReS, and TVS (Xiao et al., 2025), and DiRecT
(Giaretta et al., 2026), on the task setup of Giaretta et al. (2026). Methods requiring a nonlinear
solver use IPOPT (Wächter & Biegler, 2006). The nine constrained methods impose the keep-out
regions but leave satisfaction of the maze walls to the learned prior, and none guarantees feasibil-
ity in continuous time. Their constraints are enforced pointwise during sampling or projection, so
feasibility holds at the sampled states rather than along the whole curve.
Training and environment details. Each keep-out region is represented by an ellipse or super-
ellipse centred at (cx,cy) with semi-axes (rx,ry) and order e,

The Broad setting uses one region with e = 2 and one with e = 4, while all six keep-outs in
Narrow use e = 4. Each region straddles a wall stub and extends into the corridor, and GlobMP’s
route touches the 0.4 margin of both keep-outs in Broad and of four of the six in Narrow, as
illustrated in Figures 12 and 13. A single continuous-time diffusion model with a temporal UNet
is used by all baselines. It is trained for 500,000 steps with Adam, batch size 256, on the D4RL
maze2d-large-v1 dataset using min–max normalization. The learning rate is 10−3 with Py-
Torch’s cosine annealing (Tmax = 10,000 steps, so periodic over training), the exponential moving
average decay is 0.995, and the planning horizon is 384 steps. The model outputs a state–action
trajectory. Dynamics are identified from the same dataset by ordinary least squares over 3,993,503
transitions and are imposed on the baselines as equality constraints. Because open-loop execution
of the fitted linear model diverges over this horizon, all methods execute their planned trajectories
using a proportional–derivative controller with proportional gain 5 and derivative gain 1.
Evaluation. We follow the protocol of Giaretta et al. (2026). Each baseline generates 10 i.i.d.
trajectories conditioned on the same fixed endpoints, with each trajectory executed for at most 800
environment steps. We report SR, the fraction of collision-free rollouts; Viol., the mean number of
steps spent inside a keep-out; Score, the D4RL normalized return; and Time, the planning time. The
Gen. columns evaluate the generated trajectory before execution and additionally report acceleration
smoothness, AS, and curvature smoothness, CS (not the constraint satisfaction of Appendix A4.5).
Baseline values are means over 10 samples, whereas our method is deterministic and reports a single
trajectory.
Tables 1 and 2 reveal an important difference between planning and execution. In Broad, sev-
eral baselines generate trajectories with Gen. SR = 1.00 but lose safety during execution: Gradient
Guidance falls to 0.20, SafeDiffuser-RoS to 0.00, and Projected Diffusion, SafeDiffuser-ReS and
SafeDiffuser-TVS to 0.40. DiRecT is the only baseline that carries Gen. SR = 1.00 through execu-
tion. Both of our formulations maintain SR = 1.00 with zero violations before and after execution.
The disc-cover formulation attains a score of 1.591, while the exact e = 4 formulation improves the
score to 1.629 and achieves the lowest AS and CS. Both satisfy the keep-out constraints for every t.
The distinction becomes more pronounced in Narrow, where feasibility is difficult already at the
planning stage. Gradient Guidance, SafeDiffuser-ReS and DiRecT are the only baselines reaching
Gen. SR = 1.00, and of these only DiRecT keeps it through execution; the other two fall to 0.10. Our
trajectory maintains SR = 1.00 with zero violations, achieves a score of 1.625 compared with 1.615
for DiRecT, and obtains the lowest AS and CS, at a planning time of 90.0 s compared with 16.0 s
for DiRecT (different machines, Appendix A4.1). That time is not a single descent: Algorithm 1
is within 1% of its own final cost after 19 s and stops at 57 s (60 s cap), and an SQP polish then
supplies the final 6% of the cost (30 s, plus 3 s for the finishing step); letting Algorithm 1 run to
its own stopping rule instead (532 s) changes neither the cost nor the score. A 20 s cap gives 32.8 s
on Broad against 7.7 s for DiRecT. On both maps the returned trajectory satisfies the keep-out
constraints at every t ∈[0,1], not only at the sampled states that the benchmark checks.
In Tables 1 and 2, several baselines lose safety between the generated plan and the executed rollout,
whereas every GlobMP row is at 1.00 on both. Figures 12 and 13 show the executed rollouts of

A4.4
COLLISION-FREE OBSTACLE AVOIDANCE IN END-EFFECTOR SPACE
We evaluate GlobMP against the baselines on the constrained manipulation benchmark of Jia et al.
(2024), with GlobMP as the projection step of DiRecT’s own diffusion sampler. A planar end-
effector must reach a goal region while avoiding a set of pillars (Figure 14). The state lies in R4 and
contains the current and desired positions, while the action lies in R2 and specifies velocity. The
initial position is fixed, the goal lies on y = 0.35, and safety requires the entire trajectory to remain
outside every keep-out region. Circular keep-outs are represented by the quadratic block (10) at
degree 2d, while planar constraints use the affine block of §A2.1.1. GlobMP’s plan is a chain of
15 straight segments (C0 joins, start fixed, end free) under DiRecT’s own projection cost, which lies
outside the curve class of Theorem 1.
Baselines. We run the ten diffusion planners of the maze benchmark (§A4.3), which cover soft
guidance, projection, dual optimization, barrier-function guidance, and receding-horizon projection.
Methods requiring a nonlinear solver use IPOPT (Wächter & Biegler, 2006).
Training and environment details. A continuous-time diffusion model with a temporal UNet is
trained for 500,000 steps with Adam on the D3IL avoiding dataset using batch size 32, learning
rate 2 × 10−4, PyTorch’s cosine annealing (Tmax = 100,000 steps, so periodic over training), and
an exponential moving average with decay 0.995, at horizon H=16. The same model is shared
by all baselines, and constraints are introduced only during sampling, so no retraining is required
when the constraint set changes. The original task, learned from 96 human demonstrations, contains
six circular pillars, 0.05–0.06 m across. Following Giaretta et al. (2026), we introduce unseen test-
time constraints by enlarging the first pillar from radius 0.03 m to 0.08 m and restricting lateral
motion with two planar regions. We further activate three keep-outs of radius 0.02 m centred at
(0.5,0.16), (0.4,0.175), and (0.6,0.175), which are included in their release but disabled by default.
The resulting problem contains ten circular keep-outs and two planar constraints. These constraints
deliberately differ from the training distribution. Of the 96 demonstrations, 77 violate the enlarged
pillar, 62 violate the planar constraints, and 53 violate the three additional keep-outs. The resulting
free space contains four route classes, with a minimum passage width of 0.061 m.
Evaluation. We evaluate each method over 100 i.i.d. rollouts from the same initial position at seed
0, with at most 100 environment steps, planning horizon H=16, and execution horizon of 4 steps.
We report SR, the fraction of rollouts satisfying all constraints; TS, the fraction reaching the goal
within the episode budget; Steps(Safe), the mean steps among safe rollouts; and Time, the mean
computation time per replanning step. All values are obtained from our executions of the released
implementations.
