42


Figure 14: D3IL avoiding-v0. (a) The environment’s own view; the test-time constraints have
no physical counterpart, so they appear only in (b). (b) The scene as GlobMP sees it, with all ten
discs and both half-planes. (c) Safety rate against task success over 100 episodes, one point per
method: a point at the lower right reaches the goal without avoiding, and only the upper right does
both. Brackets as in Table 3.
GlobMP. We keep DiRecT’s checkpoint, sampler, dynamics fit and evaluation, and replace only
its projection: at every denoising step GlobMP projects the sampler’s plan, minimizing DiRecT’s
own cost, onto plans whose straight-line motion between the 16 states clears every keep-out by the
margin at every instant, not only at the states. The actions follow from the planned positions through
the dynamics fit, so their limits (the environment clips each coordinate of a step to at most 1.25 cm,
and never backwards) and their rate of change enter as linear blocks, and every planned action is
within them; the benchmark’s PD tracker (gains 5 and 1) corrects each executed action, which the
environment may still clip. A plan that starts closer to a keep-out than the margin must win the
margin back, by at most 1 cm per step. Within one plan the start state is fixed, so each projection
starts from the previous one’s solution, which is feasible when that projection succeeded.
Table 3 shows that safety and task completion do not necessarily coincide, and Figure 15 shows the
rollouts behind it. Over 100 episodes at the same seed, GlobMP is safe in 0.95 of them and reaches
the goal in 0.98, ahead of DiRecT (0.87 and 0.97), and every plan it returns is feasible at every
instant. Over four seeds (400 episodes) GlobMP averages 0.865 and 0.97, on par with DiRecT’s
single seed, and it is slower: safe rollouts take 78 steps against 61, and each plan takes longer
to compute. Projected Diffusion attains the next-highest safety rate at 0.45. In contrast, Gradient
Guidance and SafeDiffuser-TVS reach the goal in 0.97 and 0.95 of episodes but achieve safety rates
of only 0.24 and 0.02, respectively. The two metrics should therefore be interpreted jointly, since
high task success may still correspond to frequent constraint violations.
For DiRecT, the remaining safety failures occur primarily during execution rather than planning.
IPOPT converges on every solve and 815 of 819 generated plans are feasible; moreover, in 10 of the
13 unsafe episodes, every generated plan is feasible before execution. Thus, even when the planner
satisfies the test-time constraints, tracking can still drive the executed trajectory outside the feasible

A4.5
MULTI-ROBOT MOTION PLANNING
We extend the multi-robot evaluation of §4.2 to a broader benchmark with eight environments, team
sizes Na ∈{3,6,...,21}, and three test-time velocity caps (results in Figures 16–17). The benchmark
follows Shaoul et al. (2025), with the velocity constraint of Luan et al. (2026) and the evaluation
protocol of Giaretta et al. (2026). Six environments are from Shaoul et al. (2025); Corridor-Bay
and Cross-Room are reconstructed from Liang et al. (2025). Start–goal pairs follow the sampling
procedure of Shaoul et al. (2025), which places every endpoint at least 2.1r +0.01 from an obstacle
of the source map (before the enlargement below) and max(0.15,2.2r) + 0.01 from every other
endpoint.
Problem setup. Each agent follows a planar Bézier trajectory, with one block per agent and obstacle
and one per pair of agents at separation 2r (§A4.6). Box obstacles use the affine blocks of §A2.1.1,
one half-space per agent, segment and box, with the side selected by an independent grid seed.
The eight environments cover different forms of geometric and coordinated motion:
• Empty contains no obstacles and isolates inter-agent coordination.
• Basic and Dense contain ten and twenty scattered circular obstacles, respectively.
• Conveyor contains two narrow corridors with opposing preferred traffic directions, re-
quiring coordinated single-file motion.
• Highways contains a central block and eight boundary blocks that leave a ring road with
a preferred counterclockwise circulation.
• Drop-Region contains four square obstacles and rewards pausing at drop-off points be-
side them.
• Corridor-Bay contains three parallel channels joined by a narrow crossing, requiring
teams to coordinate their passage.
• Cross-Room contains a plus-shaped divider that forms four quadrants connected only
through gaps near the workspace boundary.
The demonstrations associated with each environment encode its characteristic motion pattern,
which is measured by the data-adherence metric DA; on Corridor-Bay and Cross-Room,
which have no preferred pattern, DA is the start-to-goal distance divided by the path length.
In the normalized workspace [−1,1]2, robots have radius r = 0.05 on every map except Empty,
where r = 0.10, with minimum pairwise separation 2r.
Obstacles are those of the source

der Low, Medium, High, these are (0.483,0.571,0.675) for Empty, (0.477,0.567,0.675) for

Baselines. We compare GlobMP against Diffusion Policy (Chi et al., 2023), CD, PCD-LB, and
PCD-SHD (Luan et al., 2026), Final Proj. and DiRecT (Giaretta et al., 2026), MMD-CBS (Shaoul
et al., 2025), and MDOC (He et al., 2026). Diffusion Policy provides the unguided reference; CD
uses constraint guidance; PCD-LB and PCD-SHD augment guidance with projection; Final Proj.
applies projection after sampling; MMD-CBS combines sampled single-agent motions through
conflict-based search; DiRecT applies projection throughout denoising; and MDOC uses model-
based diffusion optimal control. Each method is evaluated on the same start–goal instances.
Evaluation. We evaluate each method for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} on all eight maps and under the
three velocity caps. For each (map, team size) pair, the same 20 initial configurations are shared
across methods, and each sampling baseline generates 64 candidate trajectories per configuration
(Final Proj. samples 128 on Empty and Cross-Room, scored on the first 64 and timed over all
128); MMD-CBS and MDOC return one plan, and GlobMP one trajectory. Following Giaretta et al.
(2026), we report SR, the fraction of configurations with at least one collision-free trajectory; CS, the
fraction of all trajectories satisfying the collision and kinematic constraints; Coll, the mean number
of colliding agent pairs; DA, environment-specific motion-pattern adherence; and Time, wall-clock
time per configuration in seconds. Tables 4–11 give SR and CS in percent; the text quotes them as
fractions. A dash marks a quantity that was not measured or is not comparable, rather than a zero.
GlobMP’s CS checks collisions only, since none of its counted plans exceeds the cap. The other
conventions are restated in each table’s caption.
Figures 16–17 and Tables 4–11 summarize the results. We interpret DA together with feasibility,
since adherence to the demonstrated motion pattern is not meaningful if the generated trajectory
violates the constraints, consistent with the evaluation of Giaretta et al. (2026). At the Medium
velocity cap, averaged across all maps and team sizes, GlobMP (Ours) attains a mean SR of 0.941,

Table 5: Multi-robot planning performance on Basic for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} under the Low,
Medium and High velocity caps. SR and CS measure trajectory feasibility, Coll reports inter-robot
collisions, DA adherence to the environment-specific motion pattern, and Time the planning time per
configuration: for GlobMP (Ours) the median over the configurations with the standard deviation
as subscript, for the baselines the mean, so the two are not directly comparable. † marks a method
that applies no velocity limit, so its three blocks report the same run; MMD-CBS also returns one
plan per configuration rather than 64 candidates. A dash under Coll marks a count that is not com-
mensurable with the others, as MDOC and MMD-CBS count colliding time steps where every other
row counts agent pairs with any collision; MDOC’s CS is omitted because its plans that do not cover
every agent were counted as feasible. MMD-CBS plans under 60 s per configuration and GlobMP
(Ours) under 300 s per team.

Table 6: Multi-robot planning performance on Dense for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} under the Low,
Medium and High velocity caps. SR and CS measure trajectory feasibility, Coll reports inter-robot
collisions, DA adherence to the environment-specific motion pattern, and Time the planning time per
configuration: for GlobMP (Ours) the median over the configurations with the standard deviation
as subscript, for the baselines the mean, so the two are not directly comparable. † marks a method
that applies no velocity limit, so its three blocks report the same run; MMD-CBS also returns one
plan per configuration rather than 64 candidates. A dash under Coll marks a count that is not com-
mensurable with the others, as MDOC and MMD-CBS count colliding time steps where every other
row counts agent pairs with any collision; MDOC’s CS is omitted because its plans that do not cover
every agent were counted as feasible. MMD-CBS plans under 60 s per configuration and GlobMP
(Ours) under 300 s per team.

Table 7: Multi-robot planning performance on Conveyor for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} under the Low,
Medium and High velocity caps. SR and CS measure trajectory feasibility, Coll reports inter-robot
collisions, DA adherence to the environment-specific motion pattern, and Time the planning time per
configuration: for GlobMP (Ours) the median over the configurations with the standard deviation as
subscript, for the baselines the mean, so the two are not directly comparable. † marks a method that
applies no velocity limit, so its three blocks report the same run; MMD-CBS also returns one plan per
configuration rather than 64 candidates. A dash under Coll marks a count that is not commensurable
with the others, as MDOC and MMD-CBS count colliding time steps where every other row counts
agent pairs with any collision; MDOC’s CS is omitted because its plans that do not cover every
agent were counted as feasible. MMD-CBS plans under 60 s per configuration and GlobMP (Ours)
under 300 s per team. DA on this map scores an end-to-end traversal of one conveyor lane, which
the uniformly drawn start and goal pairs almost never require; it is therefore below 0.07 for every
method and does not separate them.

Table 8: Multi-robot planning performance on Highways for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} under the Low,
Medium and High velocity caps. SR and CS measure trajectory feasibility, Coll reports inter-robot
collisions, DA adherence to the environment-specific motion pattern, and Time the planning time per
configuration: for GlobMP (Ours) the median over the configurations with the standard deviation as
subscript, for the baselines the mean, so the two are not directly comparable. † marks a method that
applies no velocity limit, so its three blocks report the same run; MMD-CBS also returns one plan per
configuration rather than 64 candidates. A dash under Coll marks a count that is not commensurable
with the others, as MDOC and MMD-CBS count colliding time steps where every other row counts
agent pairs with any collision; MDOC’s CS is omitted because its plans that do not cover every agent
were counted as feasible. MMD-CBS plans under 60 s per configuration and GlobMP (Ours) under
300 s per team.

Table 9: Multi-robot planning performance on Drop-Region for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} under the
Low, Medium and High velocity caps. SR and CS measure trajectory feasibility, Coll reports inter-
robot collisions, DA adherence to the environment-specific motion pattern, and Time the planning
time per configuration: for GlobMP (Ours) the median over the configurations with the standard
deviation as subscript, for the baselines the mean, so the two are not directly comparable. † marks
a method that applies no velocity limit, so its three blocks report the same run; MMD-CBS also
returns one plan per configuration rather than 64 candidates. A dash under Coll marks a count that
is not commensurable with the others, as MDOC and MMD-CBS count colliding time steps where
every other row counts agent pairs with any collision; MDOC’s CS is omitted because its plans that
do not cover every agent were counted as feasible. MMD-CBS plans under 60 s per configuration
and GlobMP (Ours) under 300 s per team.

Table 10: Multi-robot planning performance on Corridor-Bay for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} under the
Low, Medium and High velocity caps. SR and CS measure trajectory feasibility, Coll reports inter-
robot collisions, DA adherence to the environment-specific motion pattern, and Time the planning
time per configuration: for GlobMP (Ours) the median over the configurations with the standard
deviation as subscript, for the baselines the mean, so the two are not directly comparable. † marks
a method that applies no velocity limit, so its three blocks report the same run; MMD-CBS also
returns one plan per configuration rather than 64 candidates. A dash under Coll marks a count that
is not commensurable with the others, as MDOC and MMD-CBS count colliding time steps where
every other row counts agent pairs with any collision; MDOC’s CS is omitted because its plans that
do not cover every agent were counted as feasible. MMD-CBS plans under 60 s per configuration
and GlobMP (Ours) under 300 s per team.

Medium (vmax = 1.46)
High (vmax = 1.76)

Table 11: Multi-robot planning performance on Cross-Room for Na ∈{3,6,...,21} under the
Low, Medium and High velocity caps. SR and CS measure trajectory feasibility, Coll reports inter-
robot collisions, DA adherence to the environment-specific motion pattern, and Time the planning
time per configuration: for GlobMP (Ours) the median over the configurations with the standard
deviation as subscript, for the baselines the mean, so the two are not directly comparable. † marks
a method that applies no velocity limit, so its three blocks report the same run; MMD-CBS also
returns one plan per configuration rather than 64 candidates. A dash under Coll marks a count that
is not commensurable with the others, as MDOC and MMD-CBS count colliding time steps where
every other row counts agent pairs with any collision; MDOC’s CS is omitted because its plans that
do not cover every agent were counted as feasible. MMD-CBS plans under 60 s per configuration
and GlobMP (Ours) under 300 s per team.

High (vmax = 1.34)

55


ahead of DiRecT at 0.601, Final Proj. at 0.419, PCD-SHD at 0.211, CD at 0.132, MMD-CBS at
0.121, PCD-LB at 0.063, MDOC at 0.025 and Diffusion Policy at 0.020.
Performance generally decreases as the number of robots increases, although the effect depends
strongly on the environment. At the Medium cap, DiRecT maintains SR = 1.00 on Empty and
Highways at every team size and at least 0.90 on Basic, but at most 0.50 on Dense. Conveyor
and Cross-Room separate the methods most sharply: the best baseline is PCD-LB, at 0.65 and 0.40
on Conveyor and 0.45 and 0.20 on Cross-Room for Na = 3 and 6, and from Na = 9 no baseline
exceeds 0.10, while GlobMP (Ours) stays at 1.00 through Na = 15 on Cross-Room and ranges
from 1.00 to 0.70 on Conveyor. Drop-Region and Corridor-Bay provide intermediate
difficulty, with DiRecT attaining mean SR values of 0.70 and 0.95, respectively; DiRecT exceeds
GlobMP on Corridor-Bay from Na = 9 (at least 0.90 against 0.60 to 0.75) and on Highways
at Na ≥18 (1.00 against 0.90 and 0.55), and edges it on Empty at Na = 21 (1.00 against 0.95).
Changing the velocity cap has a comparatively small effect (four of the nine rows, marked †, ignore
it), except for DiRecT on Drop-Region, which reaches 1.00 at every team size only under the
High cap, and for GlobMP on Highways, whose Low-cap SR falls from 0.80 at Na = 6 to 0.60
at Na = 12 and whose cells from Na = 15 hit the planning budget (0.30, 0 and 0.15), suggesting
that environment geometry and coupled collision avoidance dominate the difficulty of the harder
instances.
The GlobMP (Ours) rows use the same shared instances across all eight maps, all velocity caps,
and all 168 experimental cells, with a 300 s planning budget per team; MMD-CBS has 60 s per
configuration. Planning times are measured with one thread per job, pinned to four CPU cores. Cells
that reach the full 300 s budget were repeated on an idle machine and showed the same behaviour,
so these cases reflect planning difficulty, not resource contention: on Highways at the Low cap for
Na ≥15 and on Corridor-Bay at Na = 18 (High) and Na = 21 (Low).
The suite’s success rate is a best-of-64 quantity: an instance counts as solved when any of the 64
candidates a baseline samples is collision-free, whereas our method returns one trajectory. Figure 17
therefore scores every method with per-candidate data on the success of a single returned plan, and
Figure 16 shows the best-of-64 success rate the tables report.
A4.6
IMPLEMENTATION DETAILS
This subsection lists how GlobMP is run in the experiments: the curve class of each experiment, the
settings of Algorithm 2, the cone solve, and the checks of the implementation.
Curve class.
Table 12 gives the degree, the cost order k and the boundary order ℓof each ex-
periment; the synthetic study uses 7 pieces (4 in the scaling study). The synthetic study uses the
minimum-snap class of the baselines it is compared against (k = ℓ= 4); the multi-robot tasks pre-
scribe only where each robot starts and stops, so they fix position and velocity (ℓ= 1) and minimize
acceleration (k = 2); Maze2D uses k = ℓ= 2. In every row 2(ℓ+ 1) ≥k, so Assumption B holds,
and every degree is at least max{2k−1,ℓ}, so the synthetic class is that of Theorem 1, with rate N−1
(ℓ= k); Maze2D joins its pieces Cℓ, a subclass. Every row starts from heuristic seeds rather than
the grid start and restores with Algorithm 2 rather than the exact repair (5); the multi-robot rows add
a speed limit, and Maze2D adds corridor blocks, each box binding one piece, and keep-out blocks
outside (P∞), so the theorem certifies no row’s cost as optimal. No steering experiment (Section 4.3,
Appendix A4.4) is in the class either.

Piecewise curves.
With knots 0 = t0 < ··· < tN = 1, ∆i = ti −ti−1 and u = (t −ti−1)/∆i on piece i,
the curve is γ(t) = Γ(i)bd(u) and γ (s)(t) = ∆−s
i Γ(i)b(s)
d (u). The endpoint conditions of order s ≤ℓand
the Ck−1 (or smoother) continuity conditions at the N −1 junctions are linear in the stacked control
points, so ΓA = B gives the same orthonormal chart as for one piece, with df = N(d +1)−rank(A).
The cost matrix is blkdiag(∆1−2k
i
G(k)) (with the equal knots used here, the implementation drops the
constant factor), and the constraint blocks are built piece by piece with the derivative scaling ∆−s
i .
With these junctions the curves lie in H k, and a zero-cost perturbation is a polynomial of degree
below k, zero when k ≤2(ℓ+1).
Restoration (Algorithm 2).
The provable step 1/Lproj (Appendix A3.1.4) is too small to be prac-
tical, so the implementation calibrates the step: it starts at 10−3, caps it at 2V/∥∇V∥2 for the current
merit value V, halves it until the merit (2) decreases, and then grows it by a factor 1.3 (on Maze2D
and the Robotarium, Barzilai–Borwein steps, halved until the merit is below its largest value over
the last ten steps); blocks within their tolerance are left out of the merit and its gradient, so V and
∇V above are over the remaining blocks and the bound of Appendix A3.1.4 does not cover these
steps. In the implementation, line 3 of Algorithm 2 uses one tolerance per block: a curve is accepted
when every block satisfies ∥e j∥2 ≤tolj, with tol j = (2rjδ + δ 2)2 for a ball, pair or moving-ball
block whose radius is inflated by δ, δ 2 for a box, and 10−12 otherwise.
Cone solve.
Each projection onto Kj uses Moreau’s decomposition and fully corrective nonneg-
ative matching pursuit (Algorithm 3), with the block rescaled to unit norm. The implementation
maximizes the unnormalized correlation ⟨v,bmj(t)⟩(Algorithm 3 normalizes it, and the rate of Ap-
pendix A3.1.4 is for that version), a polynomial in t, at the real roots of its derivative and the two
endpoints (by Bernstein branch-and-bound above degree 24), then refits the nonnegative weights and
drops atoms whose weight reaches zero; it stops at the relative threshold ε = 10−6. The active-set
weight solve keeps linearly independent atoms, so at most m j + 1 (2d + 1 for a collision block of
degree 2d); the solve also stops after 2(mj +1)+10 rounds.
Finishing step.
In the synthetic study, the minimum-snap curvature ratio (15), in the chart of S7
of Proposition 13, β/α ≈4 × 107 at N = 7 makes first-order descent slow along flat directions,
so each main-comparison run is finished by Newton’s method on the first-order conditions and the
active contact equations. An interior contact at a time t∗contributes gΓ
j (t∗) = 0 and ∂tgΓ
j (t∗) = 0; the
unknowns are the free control points, the contact times and the multipliers; contacts with negative
multipliers are dropped and the system is solved again. This step lowered the cost by a further factor
of 2 to 3; the reported curves pass the dense recheck, but the step is numerical and is not covered
by the theory. As in Maze2D (§A4.3), the step follows an SQP polish of the output of Algorithm 1,
also outside the theory.
Several robots.
The free control points of all robots are stacked; the robots share d, k and ℓ, and
hence A, N and the cost matrix, and each has its own boundary data. There is one collision block per
robot and obstacle and one per pair of robots, the latter the obstacle block applied to the difference
curve Γa −Γb at the separation radius, so Algorithms 1 and 2 are unchanged. Each block reads the
control points of one or two robots only, so the gradient of Algorithm 2 costs a fixed amount per
block.
Workspace bounds.
A bound xi ≤γi(t) ≤xi is two nonnegativity conditions with Bernstein coef-
ficients Γ⊤

Checks of the implementation.
Each of the following holds to machine precision on the bench-
mark instances: Sd reproduces the Bernstein coefficients of b⊤
d Xbd; the chart satisfies ΓA = B and
N⊤A = 0; π j reproduces ∥γ(t)−cj∥2 −r2

and the cone test is exact in both directions, returning zero for collision-free curves and a positive
value, increasing with the penetration depth, for colliding ones.

Figure 18: Hardware execution on Empty: every method except PCD-LB, twelve GRITSBots on
the Georgia Tech Robotarium. The tracking controller and the virtual obstacles are those of Ap-
pendix A4.1. Each panel reports one execution of the ring instance by the method named above it:
a single overhead frame, with the trajectories recovered from the video drawn over it, each marked
by a circle at its start and a square at its terminal state. A robot is counted as being in collision when
its footprint overlaps an obstacle region. The frame shown is the midpoint of the tracked window
for an execution in which no robot overlaps a region at any time, and otherwise the frame at which
the number of overlapping robots is maximal; only the midpoints are therefore proportional to the
duration of an execution. Empty carries no obstacles, so no execution can overlap a region and
the panels differ only in the routing of the exchange, with midpoints at 25 s for GlobMP, 27 s for
PCD-SHD, 28 s for Final Proj., 29 s for DiRecT, 34 s for CD and 57 s for MMD-CBS.

Figure 19: Hardware execution on Drop-Region: the same methods, with the protocol of Fig-
ure 18. Three of the six methods complete the exchange with no footprint overlap: MMD-CBS
(midpoint frame at 61 s), DiRecT (31 s) and GlobMP (31 s). For the remaining three the panel
shows the frame of maximal overlap: two of the twelve robots overlap a keep-out region under Fi-
nal Proj. (40 s), three under CD (43 s) and five under PCD-SHD (23 s). PCD-SHD is additionally
the only execution on this map whose robots do not reach their goals; its terminal states lie well
away from the goal ring. This map discriminates least among the four maps with obstacles, and
the discrimination that remains lies in when the overlaps occur rather than in how many robots they
involve.

Figure 20: Hardware execution on Conveyor: the same methods, with the protocol of Figure 18.
The three belts sit in the middle of the arena and leave two gaps between them, so the exchange
requires an ordering of the team as well as a route; the executions differ principally in that ordering.
GlobMP completes the instance with no footprint overlap (midpoint frame at 37 s). PCD-SHD and
DiRecT each leave five of the twelve robots overlapping a belt at their frames of maximal overlap,
44 s and 65 s respectively, the latter being the latest frame on this map. CD and Final Proj. leave all
twelve overlapping, at 40 s and 48 s: both traverse the belts as a single block, so the entire team is
inside a region at one instant. MMD-CBS returned no plan on this map; its robots therefore remain
on the start ring, and its panel is marked rather than scored, since counting overlaps for a team that
never moved would record a success where no trajectory was produced.

Figure 21: Hardware execution on Corridor-Bay: the same methods, with the protocol of Fig-
ure 18. As on Drop-Region, three methods complete the exchange with no footprint overlap:
PCD-SHD (midpoint frame at 45 s), DiRecT (32 s) and GlobMP (26 s). The remaining three overlap

eight under CD (49 s). Final Proj. remains clear for the first minute and overlaps only as the team
disperses towards its goals, whereas CD overlaps from early in the execution. MMD-CBS, which
returns no plan on two of the other maps, produces one here and brings nine of the twelve robots
through without overlap.

Figure 22: Hardware execution on Cross-Room: the same methods, with the protocol of Fig-
ure 18. The cross occupies the centre of the arena and leaves four corner regions connected only
along the walls, so the exchange must be routed around the arms; a team that advances directly
towards its goals enters one of them. GlobMP is the only method that completes the instance with

the team around the arms and lose the remainder. CD and Final Proj. leave all twelve overlapping,

untouched start ring and is marked rather than scored. With Conveyor, this is the map on which
the margin between GlobMP and the baselines is largest under hardware execution.
